## GHSA-v9pv-5gc8-w2m6 · Pillow

2026-10-22 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `USE_AFTER_FREE→MEMORY`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from Pillow's multi-frame image loaders not properly invalidating or reallocating native image storage when an image's mode or size changed during a 'seek' operation to a different frame. This could lead to the reuse of stale or inappropriately sized memory buffers, particularly when transitioning between frames with different characteristics.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious multi-frame image file that, when processed by Pillow, would trigger a use-after-free condition. This could lead to denial of service (application crash), information disclosure, or potentially arbitrary code execution.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/PIL/Image.py
+++ b/src/PIL/Image.py
@@ -2746,10 +2746,16 @@ def seek(self, frame: int) -> None:
         if frame != self.tell():
             msg = "no more images in file"
             raise EOFError(msg)
 
+        if self._im is not None and (
+            self.im.size != self.size or self.im.mode != self.mode
+        ):
+            self._im = None
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a check in the base `Image.py`'s `seek` method to invalidate the `_im` (native image storage) if the image's size or mode changes. Additionally, several image plugins (Dcx, Mpo, Mic) now explicitly call the base `Image.Image.seek` method to ensure this new invalidation logic is applied, preventing the reuse of stale native image storage.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 6 occurrences**
> Languages: C#, RUST, Python, Ruby
> → GHSA-r83h-crwp-3vm7
> → GHSA-vfvv-c25p-m7mm
> → GHSA-pp85-5j63-xpq3
> → GHSA-q95x-7g78-rccv
> → GHSA-4mrv-5p47-p938

🔗 [View commit](https://github.com/python-pillow/Pillow/commit/78d97d994579aef81aafe123773d525f40f2522a)

## GHSA-33wh-fxxf-88vv · djangorestframework

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Django REST framework's content negotiation process, specifically when parsing the 'Accept' header, did not impose limits on the length or number of parameters in media types. An attacker could craft an 'Accept' header with many large quoted parameters, leading to excessive CPU consumption during parsing and content negotiation.

### Impact

An attacker could send specially crafted HTTP requests to a Django REST framework application, causing the server to spend significant CPU time (e.g., ~19 seconds per request) processing the 'Accept' header. This could lead to a denial of service, making the application unresponsive to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/rest_framework/negotiation.py
+++ b/rest_framework/negotiation.py
@@ -22,6 +22,9 @@ def select_renderer(self, request, renderers, format_suffix=None):
 class DefaultContentNegotiation(BaseContentNegotiation):
     settings = api_settings
 
+    max_accept_header_length = 8192
+    max_accept_tokens = 64
+    max_media_type_length = 256
+
     def select_parser(self, request, parsers):
         """
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces limits on the length of the 'Accept' header, the number of tokens within it, and the length of individual media types. It also optimizes the parsing of renderer media types to occur once per request instead of repeatedly. Overly long headers or media types are now truncated or skipped to prevent excessive processing.

## GHSA-3547-9m27-7rxg · djangorestframework

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Django REST framework's JSONParser allowed an attacker to specify an arbitrary codec via the 'Content-Type' header. This included byte-to-byte codecs like 'bz2_codec'. When such a codec was used, the framework would decompress the request body after the 'DATA_UPLOAD_MAX_MEMORY_SIZE' check, allowing a small compressed request to expand into a very large uncompressed one, consuming excessive memory.

### Impact

An attacker could send a small, specially crafted request that, when processed, would decompress into a massive amount of data, leading to excessive memory consumption and a denial of service for the application.

### Key diff

```
+    try:
+        ''.encode(encoding)
+    except (LookupError, UnicodeError):
+        raise ParseError(
+            'Unsupported charset "%s" in request Content-Type header.' % encoding
+        )
-        encoding = parser_context.get('encoding', settings.DEFAULT_CHARSET)
+        encoding = get_encoding(parser_context)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function, `get_encoding`, which validates the requested encoding. It explicitly checks if the encoding is a text-based codec by attempting to encode an empty string with it. If a non-text codec (like a compression codec) is specified, it raises a `ParseError`, preventing the dangerous decompression behavior. This `get_encoding` function is then used by all relevant parsers.

## GHSA-9qmh-m7c6-f92p · toml

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The `toml` parser used `BigInt` to handle integer literals without imposing a length limit. When parsing extremely long integer literals, the `BigInt` conversion process consumed excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a TOML input with an arbitrarily long integer literal, causing the parsing process to consume significant CPU time and memory, effectively leading to a denial of service for applications processing such input.

### Key diff

```
-  function peg$f45(text) {    return node('Integer', BigInt(stripUnderscores(text)), offset())  }
+  function peg$f45(text) {    return integerNode(10, text, offset())  }
...
+  function integerNode(radix, text, off) {
+    var digits = stripUnderscores(text);
+    var significant = digits.replace(/^[+-]?0*/, '');
+    if (significant.length > MAX_INT64_DIGITS[radix]) {
+      genError("Integer literal is outside the 64-bit signed integer range required by TOML.", off);
+    }
+    return node('Integer', BigInt(RADIX_PREFIX[radix] + digits), off);
+  }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `integerNode` function that checks the length of integer literals against predefined maximums for each base (binary, octal, decimal, hexadecimal) before attempting `BigInt` conversion. If a literal exceeds the maximum allowed digits for a 64-bit signed integer, an error is generated, preventing the resource-intensive conversion.

## GHSA-r77w-qph3-7vf8 · graphql-core

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The GraphQL parser had a 'max_tokens' limit to prevent resource exhaustion, but it only counted tokens that were part of the GraphQL syntax. Comment tokens, while processed and allocated memory, were not counted towards this limit, allowing an attacker to bypass the safeguard by submitting a document filled with comments.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a GraphQL query with a large number of comment tokens, causing the server to allocate excessive memory and CPU resources, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
-        token = self._lexer.advance()
+        lexer = self._lexer
+        token = lexer.advance()
+        skipped = lexer.last_token.next
+        while skipped is not None and skipped is not token:
+            self._token_counter += 1
+            skipped = skipped.next
         if token.kind is not TokenKind.EOF:
             self._token_counter += 1
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `advance_lexer` method to explicitly count all skipped comment tokens towards the `_token_counter` before checking against the `_max_tokens` limit. This ensures that documents padded with comments are correctly identified and rejected if they exceed the token limit.

## GHSA-vj8h-fx38-h3vc · graphql-core

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The 'OverlappingFieldsCanBeMerged' validation rule in GraphQL-core had a quadratic time complexity (O(n^2)) when processing queries with many fields sharing the same response name, especially when combined with inline fragments. This allowed a malicious actor to craft a query that would cause an excessive number of field comparisons, leading to high CPU usage.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted GraphQL query to a server using graphql-core, causing the server to spend a disproportionate amount of CPU time validating the query. This could lead to a denial of service, making the server unresponsive to legitimate requests.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/graphql/validation/rules/overlapping_fields_can_be_merged.py
+++ b/src/graphql/validation/rules/overlapping_fields_can_be_merged.py
@@ -620,6 +658,12 @@ def find_conflict(
     Determines if there is a conflict between two particular fields, including comparing
     their sub-fields.
     """
+    # Charge this comparison against the document-wide budget and abort validation once
+    # it is exhausted, guarding against quadratic-blowup denial of service.
+    compared_fields_and_fragment_pairs.comparisons += 1
+    if compared_fields_and_fragment_pairs.comparisons > MAX_FIELD_COMPARISONS:
+        raise ComparisonBudgetExceededError
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum budget for pairwise field comparisons (MAX_FIELD_COMPARISONS = 250,000) during the 'OverlappingFieldsCanBeMerged' validation. If this budget is exceeded, validation is aborted, and a GraphQLError is reported, preventing the quadratic complexity from consuming excessive resources. A new 'ComparisonBudgetExceededError' exception is used internally to signal when the budget is exhausted.

## GHSA-pxh4-856f-4h89 · pyjwt

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The PyJWT library's `from_jwk` function, used for processing JSON Web Keys (JWKs), allowed an empty HMAC key ('k' parameter in 'oct' type JWK) to be used for signature verification. An empty key effectively means no secret is used, making signature verification trivial to bypass.

### Impact

An attacker could forge JWT tokens signed with HS256, HS384, or HS512 algorithms by providing an 'oct' JWK with an empty key, thereby bypassing signature verification and potentially gaining unauthorized access or impersonating users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/jwt/algorithms.py
+++ b/jwt/algorithms.py
@@ -428,7 +428,10 @@ def from_jwk(jwk: str | JWKDict) -> bytes:
         if obj.get("kty") != "oct":
             raise InvalidKeyError("Not an HMAC key")
 
-        return base64url_decode(obj["k"])
+        key_bytes = base64url_decode(obj["k"])
+        if len(key_bytes) == 0:
+            raise InvalidKeyError("HMAC key must not be empty.")
+        return key_bytes
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check within the `from_jwk` function to ensure that HMAC keys (kty='oct') are not empty after base64url decoding. If an empty key is detected, an `InvalidKeyError` is raised, preventing its use in signature verification.

## GHSA-xw6w-gcfp-cf8m · djangorestframework

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The `TokenProxy` model, used for admin interface interactions, incorrectly mapped its `pk` property to the user ID instead of the actual token key. When a delete operation was attempted on a `TokenProxy` instance, the underlying Django ORM's default `delete()` method would use this incorrect `pk` (user ID) to try and match against the `key` column of the concrete `Token` model, resulting in no token being deleted. The `delete_model` method in the admin interface also attempted to manually map back to the `Token` but was still flawed.

### Impact

An attacker, or even a legitimate user, could attempt to revoke an authentication token through the Django admin interface, but the token would remain active and usable. This leads to a failure in revoking access, allowing continued unauthorized access if the token was compromised or intended to be deactivated.

### Key diff

```
--- a/rest_framework/authtoken/models.py
+++ b/rest_framework/authtoken/models.py
@@ -48,14 +48,57 @@ def __str__(self):
         return self.key
 
 
+class TokenProxyQuerySet(models.QuerySet):
+    def delete(self):
+        keys = list(self.values_list('pk', flat=True))
+        return Token.objects.using(self.db).filter(pk__in=keys).delete()
+    delete.alters_data = True
+    delete.queryset_only = True
+
 class TokenProxy(Token):
     """
     Proxy mapping pk to user pk for use in admin.
     """
+    objects = TokenProxyQuerySet.as_manager()
+
     @property
     def pk(self):
         return self.user_id
 
+    def delete(self, using=None, keep_parents=False):
+        if self.key is None:
+            raise ValueError(
+                "%s object can't be deleted because its %s attribute is set "
+                "to None." % (self._meta.object_name, self._meta.pk.attname)
+            )
+        using = using or router.db_for_write(self.__class__, instance=self)
+        deleted = type(self).objects.using(using).filter(key=self.key).delete()
+        self.key = None
+        return deleted
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a custom `TokenProxyQuerySet` and overrides the `delete()` method for both the queryset and individual `TokenProxy` instances. These new methods ensure that deletion operations correctly target the concrete `Token` model using its actual `key` attribute, rather than the user ID, effectively revoking the token. The `delete_model` method in the admin was also removed as it's no longer needed with the corrected `TokenProxy` deletion logic.

## GHSA-558c-9hv2-4prj · virtualenv

2026-10-22 · Python · **MODERATE** 4.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from virtualenv's file writing logic, which did not adequately handle symbolic links or Windows reparse points. When creating files within the virtual environment, it would overwrite existing files without first resolving whether they were symbolic links pointing outside the intended directory. This allowed an attacker to create a symlink to an arbitrary location, which virtualenv would then follow and write to.

### Impact

An attacker could create symbolic links within the virtual environment directory to arbitrary file paths on the system. When virtualenv subsequently writes to these paths (e.g., during activation script generation), it would write to the attacker-controlled external files, potentially leading to arbitrary file write or modification.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/virtualenv/util/path/_sync.py
+++ b/src/virtualenv/util/path/_sync.py
@@ -40,6 +40,12 @@ def ensure_within(root: Path, path: Path) -> None:
 
 
 def write_file(path: Path, data: bytes) -> None:
+    if path.is_symlink() or path.exists():
+        path.unlink()
+    # Exclusive creation rejects links introduced after the old entry was removed.
+    with path.open("xb") as stream:
+        stream.write(data)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `write_file` utility function that explicitly checks for and unlinks existing files or symbolic links before attempting to write. It also uses exclusive file creation (`open('xb')`) to prevent race conditions where a symlink might be introduced after the check but before the write. Additionally, a new `ensure_within` function was added to validate that all paths remain within the intended root directory and to replace any existing links or junctions.

## GHSA-3j72-p6qg-6xp7 · Pillow

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the TarIO class did not validate the 'size' field extracted from a tar archive header. If a malicious or malformed tar file contained a negative 'size' value, the calculation `(size + 511) & (~511)` could result in a non-positive offset. This non-positive offset, when used with `self.fh.seek`, would cause an infinite CPU-spin loop as the file pointer would not advance correctly.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious tar file that, when processed by Pillow's TarIO, would lead to an infinite CPU-spin loop, causing a denial of service for any application attempting to read the file.

### Key diff

```
-            self.fh.seek((size + 511) & (~511), io.SEEK_CUR)
+            offset = (size + 511) & ~511
+            if offset <= 0:
+                msg = "offset must be positive"
+                raise ValueError(msg)
+            self.fh.seek(offset, io.SEEK_CUR)
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a validation check for the calculated offset. After computing the `offset` using `(size + 511) & ~511`, it explicitly checks if `offset` is less than or equal to zero. If it is, a `ValueError` is raised, preventing the `seek` operation with a non-positive value and thus mitigating the infinite loop.

## GHSA-c4qx-w738-7hr3 · @graphql-tools/utils

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PROTOTYPE_POLLUTION→OVERRIDE`

### Root cause

The `setObjectKeyPath` function in `@graphql-tools/utils` did not adequately validate or sanitize the `keyPath` segments before using them to traverse and set properties on an object. Specifically, it allowed `__proto__` to be used as a key, which could lead to prototype pollution.

### Impact

An attacker could manipulate the prototype of JavaScript objects, potentially leading to denial of service, remote code execution in specific environments, or other unexpected behavior by injecting or modifying properties on `Object.prototype`.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/utils/src/mergeIncrementalResult.ts
+++ b/packages/utils/src/mergeIncrementalResult.ts
@@ -60,14 +60,22 @@ function setObjectKeyPath(obj: Record<string, any>, keyPath: readonly unknown[],
   let current = obj;
   let i: number;
   for (i = 0; i < keyPath.length - 1; i++) {
-    const key = keyPath[i] as string | number;
+    const key = keyPath[i];
+    if (key === '__proto__' || !isSafeObjectKey(key)) {
+      return;
+    }
     if (!hasOwnProperty(current, key) || current[key] == null) {
       current[key] = typeof keyPath[i + 1] === 'number' ? [] : {};
     }
     current = current[key];
   }
-  const finalKey = keyPath[i] as string | number;
+  const finalKey = keyPath[i];
+  if (finalKey === '__proto__' || !isSafeObjectKey(finalKey)) {
+    return;
+  }
   const existingValue = hasOwnProperty(current, finalKey) ? current[finalKey] : undefined;
   current[finalKey] = existingValue != null ? mergeDeep([existingValue, value]) : value;
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds explicit checks for `__proto__` and uses the `isSafeObjectKey` helper function for all key path segments, both intermediate and final. If an unsafe key is detected, the function now returns early, preventing the property from being set.

## GHSA-cg2g-xh64-v4q7 · dompurify

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from DOMPurify's `SAFE_FOR_TEMPLATES` feature, which was intended to prevent template expression bypasses. However, the existing sanitization logic for literal text elements (like `<style>` or `<textarea>`) did not correctly handle cases where template expressions were split across different parts of the content within an attached shadow root. Specifically, the `LITERAL_TEXT_CLOSE` regex was removed, and the logic for checking namespaces and node names was updated to use cached prototype getters, preventing attackers from overriding these properties on live nodes to bypass sanitization.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass DOMPurify's sanitization when `SAFE_FOR_TEMPLATES` was enabled, leading to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) in environments where template expressions are evaluated. This could allow arbitrary JavaScript execution in the user's browser.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/purify.ts
+++ b/src/purify.ts
@@ -14,6 +14,7 @@ import {
   arrayLastIndexOf,
   arrayPop,
      arrayPush,
+  arraySlice,
   arraySplice,
   stringMatch,
   stringReplace,
@@ -93,20 +99,6 @@ const LITERAL_TEXT_ELEMENT_NAMES = [
 ];
 const LITERAL_TEXT_ELEMENTS = freeze(addToSet({}, LITERAL_TEXT_ELEMENT_NAMES));
 
-/* Per-element end-tag matcher. On an HTML reparse the ONLY token that
-   terminates a literal-text element's raw content is its own end tag; a foreign
-   literal-text close (e.g. `</xmp>` sitting inside `<style>`) does not break
-   out, so matching is per-element, not a shared alternation. The lookahead
-   requires an HTML tag-name terminator (whitespace, `/` or `>`) so a longer
-   name such as `</styles` is not mistaken for `</style`. */
-const LITERAL_TEXT_CLOSE = (function (): Record<string, RegExp> {
-  const map: Record<string, RegExp> = {};
-  arrayForEach(LITERAL_TEXT_ELEMENT_NAMES, (name) => {
-    map[name] = seal(new RegExp('</' + name + '(?=[\t\n\f\r />])', 'i'));
-  });
-  return freeze(map);
-})();
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the `LITERAL_TEXT_CLOSE` regex, which was insufficient for handling complex template expression bypasses. It also updates the `_checkValidNamespace` function to use cached prototype getters for `namespaceURI` and `tagName` instead of direct property access. This prevents attackers from manipulating these properties on live nodes to confuse the sanitization logic and bypass security checks.

## GHSA-cg8m-r9f2-5wm2 · black

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The GitHub Action for Black directly used the 'required-version' field from 'pyproject.toml' to construct a version specifier for installing the 'black' package. This input was not sufficiently validated, allowing an attacker to inject arbitrary commands or package references into the installation command executed by the action.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve arbitrary code execution on the runner executing the GitHub Action by manipulating the 'pyproject.toml' file to include malicious package specifiers or direct references to malicious packages.

### Key diff

```
-        return version
+        if m := BLACK_VERSION_RE.match(f"black{version}"):
+            return m.group(1).strip()
+        print(
+            "::error::Invalid 'required-version' in pyproject.toml: expected a"
+            " version or version specifier, for example '26.1.0' or '>=26,<27'.",
+            file=sys.stderr,
+            flush=True,
+        )
+        sys.exit(1)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces strict validation for the 'required-version' field, ensuring it only contains valid version specifiers. It now uses a regular expression to match and extract only the version part, preventing the injection of arbitrary package names or commands.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 120 occurrences**

## GHSA-g4qv-2jxx-769h · Pillow

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from inefficient parsing of EXIF data within PNG eXIf chunks. Specifically, the original code used a `while` loop with `startswith` and slicing (`data = data[6:]`) to remove 'Exif\x00\x00' prefixes. When a malicious image contained many such prefixes, this operation led to quadratic time complexity due to repeated string reallocations and copies, causing a significant performance degradation.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious PNG image with a large number of 'Exif\x00\x00' prefixes in its eXIf chunk. Processing such an image would consume excessive CPU resources and time, leading to a denial of service for applications that process images using the Pillow library.

### Key diff

```
--- while data and data.startswith(b"Exif\x00\x00"):
---     data = data[6:]
+++ data = re.sub(b"^(Exif\x00\x00){1,2}", b"", data)
```

### Fix summary

The patch replaces the inefficient `while` loop and string slicing with a single regular expression substitution. This change ensures that all occurrences of the 'Exif\x00\x00' prefix (up to two, as per the regex) are removed in a single, more efficient operation, preventing the quadratic time complexity issue.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 249 occurrences**

## GHSA-g7cr-3p24-7wf7 · Pillow

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the buffer allocated for writing JPEG markers (like ICC profiles and comments) was not always correctly sized to account for all potential data. Specifically, when an ICC profile filled the buffer, there wasn't enough space left for the JPEG comment marker and its data, leading to an overflow when the comment was subsequently written.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious JPEG image that, when processed by Pillow, would cause a heap buffer overflow. This could lead to a denial of service (application crash) or potentially arbitrary code execution, depending on the memory layout and the attacker's control over the overflowed data.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/PIL/JpegImagePlugin.py
+++ b/src/PIL/JpegImagePlugin.py
@@ -854,11 +857,14 @@ def validate_qtables(
             bufsize += len(exif) + 5
         if extra:
             bufsize += len(extra)
+        if comment:
+            bufsize += len(comment) + 5
     else:
         # The EXIF info needs to be written as one block, + APP1, + one spare byte,
         # as libjpeg without suspension requires one byte left after writing a marker.
-        # Ensure that our buffer is big enough. Same with the icc_profile block.
-        bufsize = max(len(exif) + 5, len(extra))
+        # Ensure that our buffer is big enough. Same with the icc_profile block
+        # and the comment (COM marker).
+        bufsize = max(len(exif) + 5, len(extra), len(comment or b"") + 5)
 
     ImageFile._save(
         im, fp, [ImageFile._Tile("jpeg", (0, 0, *im.size), 0, rawmode)], bufsize
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the issue by first adding a check in Python to ensure that the comment length does not exceed the maximum allowed bytes for a marker. More importantly, it modifies the buffer size calculation to explicitly include the length of the comment (plus marker overhead) when determining the total buffer needed. Additionally, a check is added in the C code to ensure sufficient free space in the buffer before writing the comment.

## GHSA-mchm-gjx6-7h55 · @sentry/nextjs

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The previous logic for identifying Sentry tunnel requests was too broad, only checking the URL pathname. This allowed requests that matched the tunnel path but were not legitimate Sentry tunnel requests to bypass user-defined middleware, as the application would prematurely return a `Response` object mimicking `NextResponse.next()`.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a request that bypasses security middleware, potentially leading to unauthorized access to application routes or other security vulnerabilities that the middleware was designed to prevent.

### Key diff

```
-	          const isTunnelRequest = isPathnameUnderSentryTunnelRoute(url.pathname, tunnelRoute);
+	        if (req instanceof Request && isSentryTunnelRequest(req, tunnelRoute)) {
 
-	          if (isTunnelRequest) {
+	          // Create a simple response that mimics NextResponse.next() so we don't need to import Next.js internals here
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function, `isSentryTunnelRequest`, which performs a more rigorous check. It now verifies that the request method is POST and that specific query parameters ('o' and 'p') are present and contain only digits, in addition to matching the tunnel path. This ensures only legitimate Sentry tunnel requests bypass middleware.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 88 occurrences**
> Languages: Python, JavaScript, Go, PHP, C#, RUST, Java

## GHSA-mrv2-3pqr-j5jr · dompurify

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incomplete fix for a previous XSS issue. When DOMPurify's `IN_PLACE` mode was used and a hook removed a parent or sibling node, the detached subtree's event handlers remained active. This meant that even though the node was removed from the DOM, its associated JavaScript event listeners could still be triggered, leading to potential XSS.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass DOMPurify's sanitization by crafting malicious HTML that, when processed in `IN_PLACE` mode, would have its event handlers persist even after the element was removed. This could lead to the execution of arbitrary JavaScript in the user's browser, enabling session hijacking, data theft, or defacement.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/purify.ts
+++ b/src/purify.ts
@@ -576,6 +579,9 @@ function createDOMPurify(window: WindowLike = getGlobal()): DOMPurify {
    * of importing it into a new Document and returning a sanitized copy */
   let IN_PLACE = false;
 
+  /* IN_PLACE: nodes processed during the current call */
+  let IN_PLACE_VISITED: Set<unknown> | null = null;
+
   /* Allow usage of profiles like html, svg and mathMl */
   let USE_PROFILES: UseProfilesConfig | false = {};
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a mechanism to track and process nodes visited during an `IN_PLACE` sanitization run. It also strengthens the handling of element namespaces and tag names by consistently using cached prototype getters instead of direct instance properties. This prevents malicious `Object.defineProperty` overrides on live nodes from confusing the sanitization logic and ensures that all attributes and children of a detached subtree are properly stripped.

## GHSA-pjvx-prjx-h659 · nbconvert

2026-10-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The application allowed user-controlled filenames from cell output metadata to be used directly when writing files, without sanitizing path components. This meant an attacker could include '..' or '/' in the filename to write files outside the intended directory.

### Impact

An attacker could write arbitrary files to any location on the server's file system where the nbconvert process had write permissions, potentially leading to remote code execution or data corruption.

### Key diff

```
-                        filename = out.metadata["filename"]
+                        fname = out.metadata["filename"]
+                        # Sanitize: use only the basename to prevent path traversal
+                        filename = os.path.basename(fname)
```

### Fix summary

The patch sanitizes the output filename by extracting only the basename using `os.path.basename()`. It also adds logging to warn when path components are detected and removed, ensuring only the intended filename is used.

## GHSA-px85-f4q5-jh77 · @storybook/addon-mcp

2026-10-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `CORS_MISCONFIGURATION→ORIGIN`

### Root cause

The Storybook dev server, when running in development mode, did not sufficiently validate the origin of messages received via postMessage. It allowed messages from any origin, including those from a redirected iframe, which could be controlled by an attacker on the local network. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary code into the Storybook manager.

### Impact

An attacker on the local network could inject arbitrary code into the Storybook manager, potentially leading to remote code execution or other malicious activities within the developer's environment.

### Key diff

```
--- a/code/core/src/channels/postmessage/index.ts
+++ b/code/core/src/channels/postmessage/index.ts
@@ -42,6 +42,22 @@ const defaultEventOptions = { maxDepth: 25 };
     return false;
   }
 };
+
+// Only a dev-mode preview is wired to a dev server on the developer's machine, so only there is the
+// embedding manager's origin restricted. A built preview is static and may be composed by any host,
+// and a page served from an opaque origin (e.g. file://) has no origin to check against.
+const getOriginValidator = (): ((origin: string) => boolean) | null => {
+  const own = location?.origin;
+  if (globalThis.CONFIG_TYPE !== 'DEVELOPMENT' || !own || own === 'null') {
+    return null;
+  }
+  return (origin) => {
+    if (origin === own) {
+      return true;
+    }
+    try {
+      return matchesHostname(new URL(origin).hostname, LOOPBACK_HOSTNAMES);
+    } catch {
+      return false;
+    }
+  };
+};
 
 // TODO: we should export a method for opening child windows here and keep track of em.
 // that way we can send postMessage to child windows as well, not just iframe
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces origin validation for postMessage communications in development mode, ensuring that messages are only accepted from trusted origins (the manager's own origin or loopback hostnames). It also adjusts how the `refUrl` is determined to account for redirects, ensuring the correct origin is used for communication.
